Job 1:1

A certain man lived in the land of Ausitis whose name was Job. That man was true, blameless, righteous, and God-fearing, keeping away from every evil deed.

  1. Genesis 6:9 Character description parallel

    Job and Noah are both introduced as righteous and blameless men before the accounts of calamity affecting their surroundings. The parallel concerns the narrators' character judgments, not an identification of the two men.

    Read matching passage

    Genesis 6:9 These are the generations of Noah. Noah was a righteous man, blameless in his generation; Noah pleased God.

    Pinned source record

  2. Ezekiel 14:14 Explicit later reference

    Ezekiel names Job alongside Noah and Daniel as an example of righteousness, corresponding to the prologue's description of Job as righteous, blameless and God-fearing.

    Read matching passage

    Ezekiel 14:14 even if these three men—Noah, Daniel and Job—were within it, they themselves would be saved by their righteousness, says the Lord.

    Pinned source record

  3. Job 42:17b Explicit narrative identification

    The appendix again locates Job in Ausitis and adds its borders and the former name Jobab. This links the book's opening identification with its own closing identification without independently verifying the appendix's historical claims.

    Read matching passage

    Job 42:17b This is translated from the Syriac book: he lived in the land of Ausitis, on the borders of Idumea and Arabia, and his former name was Jobab.

    Pinned source record

Job 1:2

He had seven sons and three daughters.

  1. Job 42:13 Narrative restoration parallel

    The seven sons and three daughters in the opening household correspond numerically to the seven sons and three daughters born to Job at the close. The later verse does not identify them as the children who died.

    Read matching passage

    Job 42:13 Seven sons and three daughters were born to him.

    Pinned source record

Job 1:3

His livestock numbered seven thousand sheep, three thousand camels, five hundred pairs of oxen, and five hundred grazing female donkeys. He had a very large body of servants and great enterprises in the land. That man was of noble standing among those from the sunrise.

  1. Job 42:12 Narrative restoration parallel

    The closing inventory doubles each opening livestock total: sheep, camels, pairs of oxen and female donkeys. Both passages explicitly enumerate the same four categories of Job's wealth.

    Read matching passage

    Job 42:12 The Lord blessed Job’s latter things more than the former. His livestock were fourteen thousand sheep, six thousand camels, one thousand yoke of oxen and one thousand grazing female donkeys.

    Pinned source record

Job 1:4

His sons would gather with one another and hold a drinking feast each day, taking their three sisters along to eat and drink with them.

  1. Job 1:13 Same-chapter literary recurrence

    The recurring feasts involving Job's sons and daughters establish the family pattern repeated when the children gather to drink wine in their elder brother's house on the day of the losses.

    Read matching passage

    Job 1:13 When that day came, Job’s sons and his daughters were drinking wine in the house of their elder brother.

    Pinned source record

Job 1:5

When the days of the drinking feast had ended, Job would send for them and purify them, rising in the morning and offering sacrifices for them according to their number, and one calf as a sin offering for their lives. For Job said, “Perhaps my sons have conceived evil thoughts toward God in their minds.” So Job did this continually.

  1. Job 42:8 Intercession and sacrifice parallel

    Job offers sacrifices for his children because they may have entertained evil thoughts toward God; later God directs his friends to obtain an offering and prayer through Job after their false speech. Both passages present Job interceding for others.

    Read matching passage

    Job 42:8 Now take seven calves and seven rams and go to My servant Job, and he will make an offering on your behalf. My servant Job will pray for you, for I will surely accept his person. Were it not for him, I would have destroyed you; for you did not speak truth against My servant Job.”

    Pinned source record

Job 1:6

When that day came, behold, the angels of God came to stand before the Lord, and the devil came with them.

  1. 1 Kings 22:19-22 Heavenly assembly comparison

    Both scenes place heavenly beings before the Lord and include a spirit whose subsequent harmful activity occurs under an expressed commission or limit. The lying spirit in Micaiah's vision is not identified as Job's devil.

    Read matching passage

    1 Kings 22:19 Micaiah said, “Not so—not I! Hear the Lord’s word—not so! I saw the Lord, the God of Israel, sitting on His throne, and all the army of heaven stood around Him, on His right and His left.

    1 Kings 22:20 The Lord said, ‘Who will deceive Ahab king of Israel, that he may go up and fall at Remmath-gilead?’ One said this, and another said that.

    1 Kings 22:21 A spirit came forward, stood before the Lord, and said, ‘I will deceive him.’ The Lord said to it, ‘By what means?’

    1 Kings 22:22 It said, ‘I will go out and become a lying spirit in the mouth of all his prophets.’ He said, ‘You will deceive him and indeed prevail. Go out and do so.’

    Pinned source record

  2. Zechariah 3:1-2 Accusation scene comparison

    Job's devil appears in the assembly before the Lord, while Zechariah's slanderer stands beside Joshua to oppose him and receives the Lord's rebuke. Both scenes situate hostile spiritual opposition before divine authority.

    Read matching passage

    Zechariah 3:1 He showed me Joshua the high priest standing before the angel of the Lord, with the slanderer standing at his right hand to oppose him.

    Zechariah 3:2 The Lord said to the slanderer, “May the Lord rebuke you, slanderer! May the Lord, who chose Jerusalem, rebuke you! Look, is this not like a brand pulled from fire?”

    Pinned source record

Job 1:7

The Lord said to the devil, “Where have you come from?” The devil answered the Lord, “I have come after going around the earth and walking through the world beneath heaven.”

  1. 1 Peter 5:8 Adversary imagery parallel

    The devil describes traveling around the earth in Job; Peter describes the devil walking about in search of someone to devour. The shared roaming image accompanies the adversary's threatening role.

    Read matching passage

    1 Peter 5:8 Be sober; be watchful. Your adversary, the devil, walks about like a roaring lion, seeking someone to devour.

    Pinned source record

  2. Job 2:2 Repeated dialogue

    The second heavenly scene repeats the Lord's question about the devil's origin and the devil's report of walking through the world, recalling this first exchange.

    Read matching passage

    Job 2:2 The Lord said to the devil, “Where are you coming from?” Then the devil said before the Lord, “I have come after traveling through the world beneath heaven and walking through all of it.”

    Pinned source record

Job 1:8

The Lord said to him, “Have you paid attention in your mind to My servant Job? For among those on earth there is no man like him—blameless, true, God-fearing, keeping away from every evil deed.”

  1. Job 1:1 Same-chapter narrative confirmation

    The Lord's assessment of Job repeats the opening narrator's description of his innocence, truthfulness, fear of God and avoidance of evil. The coming losses are therefore not introduced as proof that Job was secretly wicked.

    Read matching passage

    Job 1:1 A certain man lived in the land of Ausitis whose name was Job. That man was true, blameless, righteous, and God-fearing, keeping away from every evil deed.

    Pinned source record

  2. Ezekiel 14:14 Explicit later reference

    The Lord calls attention to Job's outstanding righteousness here; Ezekiel subsequently names Job among three righteous men whose own righteousness would deliver them in the judgment described there.

    Read matching passage

    Ezekiel 14:14 even if these three men—Noah, Daniel and Job—were within it, they themselves would be saved by their righteousness, says the Lord.

    Pinned source record

Job 1:9

The devil answered before the Lord, “Does Job worship God for nothing?

  1. Job 2:4-5 Repeated accusation

    The first accusation asks whether Job's worship is disinterested; the second argues that preserving his life explains his endurance and proposes bodily harm. Both accusations challenge the motive behind his fidelity.

    Read matching passage

    Job 2:4 The devil replied to the Lord, “Skin for skin! Whatever a person has, he will pay for his life.

    Job 2:5 But indeed, stretch out Thy hand and touch his bones and his flesh; surely he will curse Thee to Thy face.”

    Pinned source record

  2. Job 1:21-22 Same-chapter accusation and outcome

    The devil questions whether Job worships without reward, but after losing his possessions and children Job blesses the Lord and the narrator reports no sin. The ending of this trial answers the accusation through Job's response.

    Read matching passage

    Job 1:21 “I myself came out naked from my mother’s womb, and naked I shall depart there: the Lord gave, and the Lord took away; as it seemed good to the Lord, so it happened: blessed be the name of the Lord.”

    Job 1:22 In all these things that happened to him, Job did not sin at all before the Lord or attribute foolishness to God.

    Pinned source record

Job 1:10

Hast Thou not fenced in what is outside him and what is inside his house, and everything around all that belongs to him? Thou hast blessed the works of his hands and made his livestock numerous in the land.

  1. Job 29:2-6 Retrospective description

    The devil describes divine protection around Job's household and abundant possessions; Job later remembers God's guarding care, attention to his house, children around him and former abundance.

    Read matching passage

    Job 29:2 “Who might place me in a month of former days, when God guarded me?

    Job 29:3 As when His lamp shone above my head, when by His light I walked in darkness,

    Job 29:4 when I was weighty in my ways, when God made an inspection of my house,

    Job 29:5 when I was flourishing abundantly, and my children were around me,

    Job 29:6 when my roads were flooded with butter, and my mountains were flooded with milk,

    Pinned source record

Job 1:11

But stretch out Thy hand and touch everything he has; surely he will curse Thee to Thy face.”

  1. Job 2:5 Escalating test proposal

    The first proposal predicts that taking Job's possessions will make him curse God; the second repeats that prediction while shifting the proposed blow to his bones and flesh.

    Read matching passage

    Job 2:5 But indeed, stretch out Thy hand and touch his bones and his flesh; surely he will curse Thee to Thy face.”

    Pinned source record

Job 1:12

Then the Lord said to the devil, “Behold, everything he has I give into your hand, but do not touch him.” And the devil went out from the Lord.

  1. Job 2:6 Contrasting limits on testing

    The first permission excludes touching Job himself, whereas the second hands Job over but requires preservation of his life. The two scenes state different limits on the devil's activity.

    Read matching passage

    Job 2:6 The Lord said to the devil, “Behold, I hand him over to you; only preserve his life.”

    Pinned source record

  2. Luke 22:31-32 Adversarial testing comparison

    Job's trial proceeds after the devil's proposal and the Lord's limited permission. Jesus likewise speaks of Satan demanding to sift the disciples and of His prayer that Simon's faith not fail; the outcomes and limits are not identical.

    Read matching passage

    Luke 22:31 Simon, Simon, behold, Satan demanded to have you all, to sift you like wheat.

    Luke 22:32 But I prayed for you, that your faith might not fail; and you, once you have turned back, strengthen your brothers.”

    Pinned source record

Job 1:13

When that day came, Job’s sons and his daughters were drinking wine in the house of their elder brother.

  1. Job 1:4 Same-chapter literary recurrence

    The children drinking together in the elder brother's house repeats the family feasting pattern already described, identifying the ordinary gathering interrupted by the ensuing disaster.

    Read matching passage

    Job 1:4 His sons would gather with one another and hold a drinking feast each day, taking their three sisters along to eat and drink with them.

    Pinned source record

Job 1:14

Behold, a messenger came to Job and said to him, “The pairs of oxen were plowing, and the female donkeys were grazing beside them.

  1. Job 1:3 Same-chapter inventory recall

    The first messenger names oxen and female donkeys, two of the livestock categories enumerated in Job's opening inventory. His report begins to connect the particular losses with that earlier account of wealth.

    Read matching passage

    Job 1:3 His livestock numbered seven thousand sheep, three thousand camels, five hundred pairs of oxen, and five hundred grazing female donkeys. He had a very large body of servants and great enterprises in the land. That man was of noble standing among those from the sunrise.

    Pinned source record

Job 1:15

The raiders came and carried them away, and killed the servants with swords. I alone was saved and came to report to you.”

  1. Job 1:17 Same-chapter report parallel

    Both reports describe attackers carrying off livestock, killing servants with swords and leaving one messenger to report. This edition calls the first attackers raiders and the later attackers horsemen, without identifying them as the same attacking group.

    Read matching passage

    Job 1:17 While this one was still speaking, another messenger came and said to Job, “The horsemen formed three companies against us, surrounded the camels and carried them away, and killed the servants with swords. I alone was saved and came to report to you.”

    Pinned source record

Job 1:16

While this one was still speaking, another messenger came and said to Job, “Fire fell from heaven and burned up the sheep and likewise consumed the shepherds. I alone was saved and came to report to you.”

  1. 2 Kings 1:10-12 Fire-from-heaven imagery

    Job's messenger reports fire falling from heaven and consuming sheep and shepherds; the Elijah account describes fire from heaven consuming military companies. The similar description does not establish the same cause or moral status for the victims.

    Read matching passage

    2 Kings 1:10 Elijah answered and said to the captain of fifty, ‘If I am a man of God, fire will come down from heaven and consume you and your fifty men.’ Fire came down from heaven and consumed him and his fifty men.

    2 Kings 1:11 The king again sent to him another captain of fifty with his fifty men. The captain went up and spoke to him, saying, ‘Man of God, thus says the king: Come down quickly!’

    2 Kings 1:12 Elijah answered and spoke to him, saying, ‘If I am a man of God, fire will come down from heaven and consume you and your fifty men.’ Fire came down from heaven and consumed him and his fifty men.

    Pinned source record

Job 1:17

While this one was still speaking, another messenger came and said to Job, “The horsemen formed three companies against us, surrounded the camels and carried them away, and killed the servants with swords. I alone was saved and came to report to you.”

  1. Job 1:15 Same-chapter report parallel

    The horsemen's attack repeats the earlier report's combination of stolen livestock, servants killed with swords and a sole surviving messenger, while involving camels rather than the earlier animals.

    Read matching passage

    Job 1:15 The raiders came and carried them away, and killed the servants with swords. I alone was saved and came to report to you.”

    Pinned source record

Job 1:18

While this one was still speaking, another messenger came, saying to Job, “While your sons and daughters were eating and drinking with their elder brother,

  1. Job 2:9b Narrative recollection

    The messenger recalls Job's sons and daughters eating together immediately before the collapse reported next; Job's wife later mourns those sons and daughters as the vanished memorial for whom she labored.

    Read matching passage

    Job 2:9b For behold, your memorial has vanished from the earth: sons and daughters, the pangs and pains of my womb, for whom I labored in vain with hardships.

    Pinned source record

Job 1:19

a great wind suddenly came from the wilderness and struck the four corners of the house. The house fell on your children, and they died. I alone was saved and came to report to you.”

  1. Job 2:9b Narrative recollection

    The house's collapse kills Job's children; his wife's later lament specifically recalls the loss of the sons and daughters she bore. Her speech gives the mother's perspective on this reported catastrophe.

    Read matching passage

    Job 2:9b For behold, your memorial has vanished from the earth: sons and daughters, the pangs and pains of my womb, for whom I labored in vain with hardships.

    Pinned source record

  2. Job 42:13 Loss and later restoration

    The deaths of Job's children precede the closing report that seven sons and three daughters were born to him. The connection follows the household's loss and later renewal without asserting that the later children are the same individuals.

    Read matching passage

    Job 42:13 Seven sons and three daughters were born to him.

    Pinned source record

Job 1:20

Then Job rose, tore his garments, and cut off the hair of his head. He fell to the ground, bowed in worship, and said:

  1. Job 2:12 Mourning gesture parallel

    Job tears his garments on receiving the reports, and his friends later tear their robes and weep when they see his condition. Both passages portray mourning through torn clothing.

    Read matching passage

    Job 2:12 When they saw him from far away, they did not recognize him. Crying out loudly, they wept, each tearing his own robe and sprinkling earth upon himself.

    Pinned source record

  2. Ezra 9:3 Mourning gesture comparison

    Both Job and Ezra tear their garments and remove hair from their heads in distress. Job cuts his hair after bereavement, while Ezra plucks hair after hearing of covenant-breaking, so the shared gestures have different occasions.

    Read matching passage

    Ezra 9:3 When I heard this word, I tore my garments, shook with agitation, plucked hair from my head and beard, and sat quietly.

    Pinned source record

Job 1:21

“I myself came out naked from my mother’s womb, and naked I shall depart there: the Lord gave, and the Lord took away; as it seemed good to the Lord, so it happened: blessed be the name of the Lord.”

  1. Ecclesiastes 5:14-15 Close verbal and thematic parallel

    Both passages describe coming naked from the mother's womb and departing as one came. Ecclesiastes emphasizes that no earnings can be carried away, corresponding to Job's acknowledgment that what was given can be taken.

    Read matching passage

    Ecclesiastes 5:14 As he came naked from his mother’s womb, he will return to depart as he came. He will take nothing from his toil to carry in his hand.

    Ecclesiastes 5:15 This too is a grievous sickness: just as he came, so he will depart. What surplus has he from toiling for the wind?

    Pinned source record

  2. 1 Timothy 6:7 Possessions and mortality parallel

    Job contrasts his naked arrival and departure with the Lord's giving and taking; First Timothy says that nothing was brought into the world and nothing can be carried out. Both detach possessions from the beginning and end of life.

    Read matching passage

    1 Timothy 6:7 For we brought nothing into the world, because neither can we carry anything out.

    Pinned source record

Job 1:22

In all these things that happened to him, Job did not sin at all before the Lord or attribute foolishness to God.

  1. Job 2:10 Repeated narrative judgment

    After the first losses the narrator says Job did not sin or attribute foolishness to God; after bodily affliction the narrator again reports that he did not sin with his lips before God.

    Read matching passage

    Job 2:10 He looked at her and said, “You have spoken like one of the foolish women. If we received good things from the Lord’s hand, shall we not bear the bad things?” In all these things that happened to him, Job did not sin at all with his lips before God.

    Pinned source record

  2. James 5:11 Explicit later reference

    The narrator's judgment that Job remained without sin through these losses supplies an episode of the endurance James explicitly recalls when naming Job and the Lord's compassionate outcome.

    Read matching passage

    James 5:11 Look, we call those who endured blessed. You heard of Job’s endurance and saw the outcome from the Lord, because the Lord is full of compassion and merciful.

    Pinned source record

Job 2:1

When that day came, the angels of God came to stand before the Lord, and the devil came among them to stand before the Lord.

  1. Job 1:6 Repeated heavenly scene

    The second scene repeats the angels' appearance before the Lord and the devil's presence among them, reopening the setting in which the first trial was authorized.

    Read matching passage

    Job 1:6 When that day came, behold, the angels of God came to stand before the Lord, and the devil came with them.

    Pinned source record

Job 2:2

The Lord said to the devil, “Where are you coming from?” Then the devil said before the Lord, “I have come after traveling through the world beneath heaven and walking through all of it.”

  1. Job 1:7 Repeated dialogue

    The Lord again asks where the devil has come from, and the devil again reports traveling through the world. The repeated exchange frames the second challenge as a continuation of the first.

    Read matching passage

    Job 1:7 The Lord said to the devil, “Where have you come from?” The devil answered the Lord, “I have come after going around the earth and walking through the world beneath heaven.”

    Pinned source record

Job 2:3

The Lord said to the devil, “Have you then paid attention to My servant Job? For among those on earth there is no man like him—innocent, true, blameless, God-fearing, keeping away from every evil. He still holds to innocence, but you said to destroy his belongings for nothing.”

  1. Job 1:8 Repeated assessment with development

    The Lord again describes Job's exceptional innocence and fear of God, now adding that he still holds his innocence after the destruction of his possessions. The earlier assessment is expressly maintained after the first test.

    Read matching passage

    Job 1:8 The Lord said to him, “Have you paid attention in your mind to My servant Job? For among those on earth there is no man like him—blameless, true, God-fearing, keeping away from every evil deed.”

    Pinned source record

Job 2:4

The devil replied to the Lord, “Skin for skin! Whatever a person has, he will pay for his life.

  1. Matthew 16:26 Life and possessions comparison

    The devil claims that a person will pay whatever he possesses to preserve his life; Jesus asks what profit the whole world offers if life is forfeited and what can be given in exchange for life. The valuation is comparable without endorsing the devil's accusation.

    Read matching passage

    Matthew 16:26 For what will a person gain if he gains the whole world but forfeits his life? Or what will a person give in exchange for his life?

    Pinned source record

Job 2:5

But indeed, stretch out Thy hand and touch his bones and his flesh; surely he will curse Thee to Thy face.”

  1. Job 1:11 Escalating test proposal

    The prediction that Job will curse God repeats the first proposal, but the object of the requested blow changes from possessions to bones and flesh. This identifies the second trial's escalation.

    Read matching passage

    Job 1:11 But stretch out Thy hand and touch everything he has; surely he will curse Thee to Thy face.”

    Pinned source record

Job 2:6

The Lord said to the devil, “Behold, I hand him over to you; only preserve his life.”

  1. Job 1:12 Contrasting limits on testing

    The Lord now permits harm to Job but requires that his life be preserved. The earlier permission placed his possessions in the devil's hand while forbidding harm to Job himself.

    Read matching passage

    Job 1:12 Then the Lord said to the devil, “Behold, everything he has I give into your hand, but do not touch him.” And the devil went out from the Lord.

    Pinned source record

Job 2:7

The devil went out from the Lord and struck Job with a grievous ulcer from his feet to his head.

  1. Deuteronomy 28:35 Close bodily description with contextual difference

    Both passages describe a grievous ulcer extending from the feet toward the head. Deuteronomy places it among covenant curses, whereas Job's prologue explicitly calls him innocent; the shared description does not prove Job's guilt.

    Read matching passage

    Deuteronomy 28:35 The Lord smite you upon the knees and upon the legs with a grievous ulcer, so that you cannot be healed, from the soles of your feet unto the crown of your head.

    Pinned source record

  2. 2 Corinthians 12:7 Adversarial bodily affliction comparison

    Job's ulcer is inflicted by the devil, while Paul describes a thorn in the flesh and a messenger of Satan striking him. Both connect affliction with hostile spiritual agency, but Paul's unspecified thorn is not identified as Job's ulcer.

    Read matching passage

    2 Corinthians 12:7 And because of the surpassing revelations, therefore, so that I might not be exalted, a thorn was given me in the flesh, a messenger of Satan, to strike me, so that I might not be exalted.

    Pinned source record

Job 2:8

    The narrator describes Job scraping discharge with a potsherd; Job later describes his decaying body and scraping off its discharge. The later complaint recalls the same distinctive bodily action.

    Read matching passage

    Eliphaz says true words would have prevented Job's evils. The closing divine judgment instead rebukes Eliphaz and his friends for failing to speak truth before God and against Job. That verdict checks the accusation rather than establishing Job's guilt.

  1. Job 14:10-15 Death's finality and a later hope of return

    Job here says a person descending to Hades never comes up again. His later speech first describes death and not rising, then wishes for protection in Hades and says he will wait until he comes to be again. The later hope is retained rather than suppressing it to make both passages a single unqualified denial of return.

    Psalms 38:13 Hear my prayer, Lord, and give ear to my supplication; do not remain silent at my tears. For I am a sojourner with Thee and a temporary resident like all my fathers.

  1. Job 9:33 Recurring request for a hearing between human and God

    Job wishes for a hearing before the Lord and for a son of man to plead for a neighbor. Earlier he wished for a mediator to hear the cause between both sides. The link compares the desired intercession without naming the unspecified advocate.

    Job says he is perishing and needs burial but cannot obtain it. His first lament described people longing for death without obtaining it and rejoicing if they do. Both portray desired release as unavailable, while death and burial are not treated as identical requests.

    Zophar associates ungodly delight with a terrible fall and lawless joy with destruction. Job's next reply describes wicked people living long, prospering and resting in Hades; this contrast does not add a brief-duration phrase absent from Zophar's line.

Job 20:9

An eye glanced past him and will not do so again, and his place will no longer perceive him.

  1. Job 7:10 A place no longer recognizes the absent person

    Zophar applies a place's failure to perceive someone to the doomed wicked person. Job earlier used his place's failure to recognize him within his own death lament; the shared image does not make Job's death lament a confession of wickedness.

    Read matching passage

    Job 7:10 nor will he ever return again to his own house, nor will his place recognize him any longer.

Job 20:11

His bones were filled with his youth, and it will lie with him upon the mound.

  1. Job 21:23-26 Bodily vigor does not prevent burial

    Zophar sends youthful bones to the mound. Job's reply describes a vigorous, prosperous person and a bitter sufferer both sleeping in earth and decay; the reply broadens burial beyond the wicked person of Zophar's warning.

    Read matching passage

    Job 21:23 This one will die in the strength of his integrity, entirely comfortable and prosperous;

    Job 21:24 his inward parts are full of fat, and his marrow spreads abundantly.

    Job 21:25 But another dies in bitterness of soul, never having eaten anything good.

    Job 21:26 Together they sleep upon the earth, and decay covered them.

Job 20:12

If evil becomes sweet in his mouth, he will hide it under his tongue;

  1. Job 20:14 Same-chapter reversal from sweet evil to poisonous digestion

    The sweetness of evil hidden under the tongue becomes asps' gall in the belly. These distinct stages within Zophar's extended eating image contrast initial attraction with its asserted harmful result.

    Read matching passage

    Job 20:14 And he will certainly not be able to help himself; an asp’s gall is in his belly.

Job 20:14

And he will certainly not be able to help himself; an asp’s gall is in his belly.

  1. Deuteronomy 32:33 Moral corruption expressed through asp venom

    Zophar pictures evil becoming an asp's gall in the belly. Deuteronomy describes corrupt wine as serpent and asp venom; the poisonous-food imagery is compared without equating the drink, people or occasion.

    Read matching passage

    Deuteronomy 32:33 Their wine is the venom of serpents, and the incurable venom of asps.

  1. Jeremiah 28:44 Unjust acquisition pictured as forced disgorgement

    Zophar says unjust wealth is vomited out and that an angel will drag it from the person's house. Jeremiah pictures Babylon being made to disgorge what she swallowed; the shared expulsion image does not identify the owner, angel or city across passages.

    Read matching passage

Job 20:18

  1. Ecclesiastes 6:1-2 Possessions whose owner cannot enjoy them

    Zophar describes labor and wealth that cannot be tasted, chewed or swallowed. Ecclesiastes describes someone granted wealth but not empowered to eat from it; both distinguish possession from enjoyment, without making their explanations identical.

    Ecclesiastes 6:2 a person to whom God will give wealth, possessions and glory, so that his soul lacks nothing it desires, yet God will not empower him to eat from it, for a stranger will eat it. This is futility and a grievous sickness.

Job 20:22

  1. Luke 12:19-20 Assumed sufficiency interrupted by sudden loss

    Zophar places distress at the moment a man believes himself filled. The rich fool likewise announces ample stores and repose immediately before his life is demanded; this is a comparison of false security, not the same person or event.

Job 21:26

Together they sleep upon the earth, and decay covered them.

  1. Ecclesiastes 9:2-3 Different lives converge in death

    Job's two differently situated people sleep alike in earth and decay. Ecclesiastes similarly describes one event reaching people across moral and ritual distinctions; the comparison distinguishes Job's contrasting life conditions from Ecclesiastes' explicit classifications.

    Read matching passage

    Ecclesiastes 9:2 There is futility in everything: one event comes to the righteous and the ungodly, to the good and the bad, to the clean and the unclean, to the one who sacrifices and the one who does not sacrifice.     Ecclesiastes 9:3 This is an evil in all that is done under the sun: one event comes to everyone. Moreover, the heart of human beings is full of evil, and delirium is in their heart during their lives; afterward they go to the dead.

    God promises to accept Job's prayer for the friends. James teaches praying for one another and attributes great strength to a righteous person's petition, offering a corresponding statement about effective intercession.

    Read matching passage

    James 5:16 Therefore confess your sins to one another and pray for one another, so that you may be healed. A righteous person’s petition has great strength when it is at work.

    Pinned source record

Job 42:9

Eliphaz the Temanite, Bildad the Shuhite and Zophar the Minaean went and did as the Lord commanded them, and He released their sin through Job.

  1. Job 1:5 Job intercedes for others

    The friends obey and their sin is released through Job. The prologue described Job repeatedly offering sacrifices over his children's possible wrongdoing, establishing his earlier role in seeking cleansing on behalf of others.

    Read matching passage

    Job 1:5 When the days of the drinking feast had ended, Job would send for them and purify them, rising in the morning and offering sacrifices for them according to their number, and one calf as a sin offering for their lives. For Job said, “Perhaps my sons have conceived evil thoughts toward God in their minds.” So Job did this continually.

Job 42:10

The Lord increased Job. When he also prayed for his friends, He forgave their sin. The Lord gave double what Job had formerly, for a doubling.

  1. James 5:11 explicit later reflection on Job

    The Lord restores Job while forgiving his friends through his prayer. James explicitly recalls Job's endurance and the outcome from the compassionate Lord, giving a named later reflection on Job's reversal.

    Read matching passage

    James 5:11 Look, we call those who endured blessed. You heard of Job’s endurance and saw the outcome from the Lord, because the Lord is full of compassion and merciful.

Job 42:11

All his brothers and sisters heard everything that had happened to him and came to him, along with all who had known him from the beginning. They ate and drank with him and consoled him, and marveled at all the Lord had brought upon him. Each gave him one ewe lamb and an unstamped gold piece of four drachmas.

  1. Job 19:13-19 reversal of social estrangement

    Brothers, sisters and former acquaintances come to console and eat with Job. His lament had described estranged relatives, acquaintances and household relations, making their restored company a specific reversal.

    Read matching passage

    Job 19:13 My brothers withdrew from me; they recognized strangers rather than me, and my friends became merciless.

    Job 19:14 Those nearest me did not acknowledge me, and those who knew my name forgot me.

    Job 19:15 Neighbors of the house and my maidservants— I was a foreigner before them.

    Job 19:16 I called my servant, but he did not answer; my mouth made entreaty.

    Job 19:17 I entreated my wife and called with flattering words to the sons of my concubines.

    Job 19:18 They disowned me for ever; whenever I rise, they speak against me.

    Job 19:19 Those who knew me abhorred me; those I had loved rose against me.

Job 42:12

The Lord blessed Job’s latter things more than the former. His livestock were fourteen thousand sheep, six thousand camels, one thousand yoke of oxen and one thousand grazing female donkeys.

  1. Job 1:3 doubled livestock inventory

    The closing inventory gives fourteen thousand sheep, six thousand camels, a thousand ox-yokes and a thousand female donkeys. The opening inventory records exactly half those quantities for each of the four classes.

    Read matching passage

    Job 1:3 His livestock numbered seven thousand sheep, three thousand camels, five hundred pairs of oxen, and five hundred grazing female donkeys. He had a very large body of servants and great enterprises in the land. That man was of noble standing among those from the sunrise.

Job 42:13

Seven sons and three daughters were born to him.

  1. Job 1:2 family-count correspondence

    Seven sons and three daughters are born in the restoration narrative, matching the opening household's numbers. The correspondence concerns the counts; it does not identify the later births as the earlier children raised from death.

Job 42:15

None were found beneath heaven better than Job’s daughters, and their father gave them an inheritance among their brothers.

  1. Numbers 27:7-8 inheritance conditions contrasted

    Job gives his daughters an inheritance among their brothers. Numbers grants daughters inheritance when a father has no son; both concern daughters inheriting, but Job's explicit brothers make the legal situations different.

    Read matching passage

    Numbers 27:7 The daughters of Zelophehad have spoken rightly. You shall surely give them a possession of inheritance among their father’s brethren, and shall assign their father’s allotment unto them.

    Numbers 27:8 And you shall speak unto the sons of Israel, saying: If a man dies, and has no son, you shall assign his inheritance unto his daughter.

Job 42:16

After the affliction, Job lived one hundred seventy years; in all, he lived two hundred forty-eight years. Job saw his sons and his sons’ sons, a fourth generation.

  1. Genesis 50:23 seeing later generations

    Job lives to see a fourth generation of descendants. Genesis says Joseph saw Ephraim's descendants to a third generation, providing another explicit notice of long life measured through descendants without equating the ages or generations.

    Read matching passage

    Genesis 50:23 And Joseph saw Ephraim’s children to the third generation; and the sons of Machir, Manasseh’s son, were born upon Joseph’s thighs.

Job 42:17

Job died elderly and full of days.

  1. Genesis 25:8 death notice formula

    Job dies elderly and full of days. Abraham's death notice likewise calls him old and full of days, sharing the closing life-description without implying that the two lifespans were equal.

    Read matching passage

    Genesis 25:8 And Abraham’s strength failed, and he died in a good old age, an old man and full of days; and he was added to his people.

    Pinned source record

  2. 1 Chronicles 29:28 death notice formula

    Job's old age and fullness of days at death correspond to David's death in a good old age, full of days. Chronicles additionally names wealth and glory, which are not substituted into Job's brief notice.

    Read matching passage

    1 Chronicles 29:28 He died in a good old age, full of days, wealth, and glory. Solomon his son reigned in his place.

Job 42:17a

It is written that he will rise again with those whom the Lord raises.

  1. Daniel 12:2 resurrection expectation

    Job's appended unit explicitly states that he will rise with those the Lord raises. Daniel likewise describes sleepers in the earth arising, while distinguishing their outcomes; the comparison does not erase those distinctions.

    Read matching passage

    Daniel 12:2 Many of those sleeping in the breadth of the earth shall arise: some unto everlasting life, others unto reproach, and others unto dispersion and everlasting shame.

    Pinned source record

  2. John 5:28-29 resurrection expectation

    The appendix places Job among those the Lord will raise. Jesus describes those in tombs hearing His voice and coming out to resurrection, including differing judgments; both explicitly speak of the dead being raised.

    Read matching passage

    John 5:28 Do not marvel at this, because an hour is coming in which all who are in the tombs will hear His voice

    John 5:29 and come out: those who did good things to a resurrection of life, but those who practiced base things to a resurrection of judgment.

Job 42:17b

This is translated from the Syriac book: he lived in the land of Ausitis, on the borders of Idumea and Arabia, and his former name was Jobab.

  1. Genesis 36:33 appended identification and royal list

    The appendix explicitly gives Job the former name Jobab and places him near Idumea. Genesis lists Jobab as an Edomite king. This records the appendix's asserted identification and the matching royal entry, not independent historical verification.

    Read matching passage

    Genesis 36:33 And Balak died, and Jobab the son of Zerah from Bozrah reigned in his place.

Job 42:17d

These are the kings who reigned in Edom, the land he himself also ruled: first Balak son of Beor, whose city was called Dennaba; after Balak, Jobab, called Job; after him, Asom, a ruler from the land of Teman; after him, Adad son of Barad, who cut down Midian in the plain of Moab, whose city was called Geththaim.

  1. Genesis 36:31-35 parallel Edomite royal sequence

    The appendix's sequence Balak, Jobab, Asom and Adad corresponds to Genesis' successive Edomite rulers, including the named fathers, cities and victory over Midian. The source's spellings and explicit Jobab-Job identification remain intact.

    Read matching passage

    Genesis 36:31 And these are the kings who reigned in Edom before a king reigned in Israel.

    Genesis 36:32 And Balak the son of Beor reigned in Edom, and the name of his city was Dennaba.

    Genesis 36:33 And Balak died, and Jobab the son of Zerah from Bozrah reigned in his place.

    Genesis 36:34 And Jobab died, and Asom from the land of the Temanites reigned in his place.

    Genesis 36:35 And Asom died, and Hadad the son of Barad, who struck down Midian in the plain of Moab, reigned in his place; and the name of his city was Geththaim.

    Pinned source record

  2. 1 Chronicles 1:43-46 parallel Edomite royal sequence

    Chronicles repeats the appendix's four-king sequence, with corresponding fathers, cities and the defeat of Midian in Moab. The texts retain their own name spellings; the connection concerns the detailed sequence rather than an invented harmonized genealogy.

    Read matching passage

    1 Chronicles 1:43 These are their kings: Balak son of Beor, whose city was named Dennaba.

    1 Chronicles 1:44 Balak died, and Jobab son of Zara from Bosorra became king in his place.

    1 Chronicles 1:45 Jobab died, and Asom from the land of the Thaimans became king in his place.

    1 Chronicles 1:46 Asom died, and Adad son of Barad, who struck Midian in Moab’s plain, became king in his place. His city was named Geththaim.

The friends who came to him were Eliphaz, from the sons of Esau, king of the Temanites; Bildad, ruler of the Shuhites; and Zophar, king of the Minaeans.

    The appendix lists Eliphaz, Bildad and Zophar with the same Temanite, Shuhite and Minaean royal roles as their arrival notice. The opening notice does not independently establish the appendix's additional Esau ancestry.
